i-7783f4acbf7a65e2-1	Mr President, I would like to The Swedish Parliament is today to decide on the Government's proposal to introduce a provision on amortisation requirements in the Banking and Financing Business Act. This means that when banks and other credit institutions lend money to finance a home, they must also have conditions under which the borrower will normally amortise – pay back – on his loan. A robust amortisation culture strengthens households' financial resilience. It is a cornerstone of sound credit operations and, at the same time, helps to keep debt accumulated at levels that do not expose society to macro-economic risks. The background to this legislation is, of course, the increase in indebtedness that we can see in Swedish households. Since the mid-1990s, household debt has grown much faster than income. The debt-to-GDP ratio has not really, but almost doubled during this period. An important explanation for the rise in debt is, of course, the rise in prices that has simultaneously characterized our housing market. A lot of this can be attributed to structural factors such as sales, conversions, rents, changes in the tax system and a substantial reduction in interest rates. Nevertheless, according to Finansinspektionen, a large part remains, about a quarter of the increase in the debt ratio, which cannot be explained structurally. And even if Swedish households have margins to meet interest expenses even if interest rates were to be raised properly, income would fall or house prices would fall, high loan risk would be macroeconomic. In order to cope with major disruptions, households would have to reduce their consumption, with negative effects on growth and unemployment. International studies also show that households with indebtedness above 50% of the market value of the dwelling are more likely to reduce their consumption in the event of unforeseen events. Since Sweden has had a weak amortisation culture and still has long repayment periods, where many with debts over 50 per cent do not amortise at all or only amortise a little, there is good reason to work with legislation for a better order in the future. International organisations such as the OECD, the IMF and the European Commission also clearly point in their reviews to the private debt build-up as a risk in and for the Swedish economy. The proposal now partially approved by the committee is based on an agreement between the government and five other parties. During the committee's work, we have changed the proposal somewhat, which actually makes it even more robust. In particular, the Committee on Finance wants to limit the exemption for amortisation requirements in new production to five years. We can therefore limit the lock-in effects that the amortisation of the first owner of new production risks. I would also like to make it clear here that the derogation does not provide for a legislative grace period for new production. The banks can, and should probably also quite often, still require the borrower to amortise. The committee also stresses, like the government in the bill, that forestry and agricultural property should not be covered and that, as in the past, these requirements should apply to new credits, not to old loans. It should also be possible to obtain an exemption from amortisation if, for example, a person suffers sickness or divorce or a loved one disappears. Amorisation requirements, of course, are not the only answer to household debt build-up and an increasingly tough situation in the housing market. Perhaps the most important effort is to increase housing from the low levels we have had in the last decade. Especially important is that rental apartments are built. The government has also proposed measures to increase the supply of housing. The largest investment in housing policy in a few decades was launched last spring and covers over SEK 11 billion in 2016 and 2017. Through investment support, resources can also be directed towards the construction of more affordable rental and affordable rental apartments so as to reduce pressure on the housing market as well as the need for households to go into debt. In addition, the government has set aside money to give municipalities clear incentives to contribute to increased housing construction. Mr President, I would like to Today, however, the committee proposes that we introduce a provision on amortisation requirements. I support the committee's proposal in the report.
i-7783f4acbf7a65e2-15	Mr President, I would like to Today we are dealing with the bill on amortisation requirements. There have been many tours, but now a united committee is behind these proposals. The change is implemented in the Banking and Finance Business Act and is thus addressed to credit institutions. The reason why we want to implement amortisation requirements is that we see that household debt has increased significantly. People's debt in relation to disposable income has increased, and house prices have risen at a rapid pace. Several heavy international assessors have pointed out the weak amortisation culture as a problem and recommended Sweden to increase the amortisation rate of mortgages and to introduce a amortisation requirement. Mr President, I would like to When my parents were new homeowners and borrowers, it was obvious that there was a mortgage. You'd reduce your debts. You never know what's going on. At that time, interest rates were still as high as 16 percent during certain periods. Still, debts were grazed. In recent years, amortisation has not been nearly as obvious. In September 2011, only 44 per cent of households with a loan ratio of between 70 and 85 per cent amortised. Many, especially in metropolitan areas, relied on a substantial increase in value and thus did not see the need to amortise. Then the risks will also be greater. If the price rise fails, the margins will be smaller. If prices fall, those who have to sell quickly may end up in long-standing economic problems. I don't think many people really think that could happen. But it was not long ago that the bubble burst in Copenhagen. Prices had been pushed up quickly, and expectations were high. Then came the financial crisis at the same time as the construction started. Even if you do not see such a clear risk here and now, you still need to have margins for the unforeseen. Amortisation requirements have also become tougher on the banks' side in recent times. Nowadays, banks for new borrowers usually require a rather large-scale amortisation. Now 94 per cent of households with new loans and a loan ratio of between 70 and 85 per cent are amortising. At the same time, clear guidelines with tough amortisation requirements from the Bankers' Association to the banks have been blocked and have been rolled back for competition reasons. The banks therefore also see the need for regulatory regulation and are broadly in favour of the legislative proposal now being implemented. The Bankers' Association believes that without a statutory requirement, it cannot be excluded that we fall back into a situation where a significant proportion of borrowers do not amortise their mortgages, which would reinforce the risks already present on the Swedish housing market. With self-regulation on amortisation, there would have been a lack of flexibility in legal requirements. However, large parts of the actual application of the amortisation requirement will be governed by regulations from Finansinspektionen and the Government, not by law. It is extremely important that the government closely follows the effects of the regulations so that there are no unexpected or unwanted effects. I also think that there is reason to follow developments and see how different reforms affect each other in relation to each other. The more taps you turn, the more power you can get. Mr President, I would like to I would like to highlight a couple of important exceptions to the amortisation requirement. When people encounter something unexpected, such as sickness, separation or unemployment that can have quite a major economic impact, it is important to make a temporary break in the amortisation. For example, those who intend to sell their home in order to adapt to a new economic situation should have time to do so without incurring unnecessary debts in the meantime. Another very important exception is what applies to agricultural and forestry properties. In its position, the committee further stresses the importance of these being completely exempted from the amortisation requirement – something that the government has already written in the bill. There should be no room for them or parts of them to be included, as this would involve major administrative difficulties and difficult boundaries. Rules are something companies need less of and no more. For us, it has also been important to counteract retroactivity. It is important that those who have already concluded loan agreements on certain premises can expect them to continue to apply. After the Law Council has pointed out that it should be clearly stated in the Act that it only applies to new credits signed after the Act's entry into force, the Committee has adopted the Legislative Council's proposal for clarification. There have been objections that, despite this, there may be retroactiveity when borrowers take out additional loans. For them, the amortisation rate would be based on the full leverage ratio. And there has been concern that this may lead households to opt for a blank loan instead of using the home as collateral. This is an example of an effect that we expect the government to follow very closely. The bill proposes an eternal exemption from amortisation for newly produced dwellings. There was political agreement behind the proposal because we want to encourage new construction. However, several referrals were very negative. Many argued that there was a risk of significant price falls when newly produced apartments were then resold and favoured at least a time limit in the exemption. We have therefore agreed in committee to set a five-year limit for the exemption from amortisation. We are not supposed to create new problems while trying to remedy others. Overall, we believe that an amortisation requirement can contribute to a healthier amortisation culture, which in turn increases the household's resilience and makes them financially more resilient to various disruptions. It is the responsibility of the government to monitor developments after the amortisation requirement has started to apply and, if necessary, to adjust the regulations so that there are no undesirable effects. With this, Mr President, I would like to support the committee's proposal for a decision.

Then it is not a question of our sovereign debt being large or our companies being heavily leveraged. No, it is household indebtedness that has run out in recent years. The balance over household indebtedness has been pointing upwards at an unbroken pace. According to Statistics Sweden, SCB, households' loans have increased by SEK 1,100 billion since the beginning of 2008, or by 56 per cent. When it takes this long for the policy to react and even introduce such a small barrier to debt as an amortisation requirement, you wonder if the policy is capable of delivering. The process of introducing an amortisation requirement has taken over a year, but the debate and proposals have been much longer. Sweden is a small and open economy. This means that we are quickly affected by decisions in the world around us. It also requires us to be able to both decide on preventive measures in good time and to take rapid decisions when necessary. The question is whether we have the political capacity. Another question we politicians have to ask ourselves is whether the amortisation requirement is sufficient. Five years ago, the requirement for self-contribution was increased from 10 per cent to 15 per cent of the cost of a home purchase. It had a small impact, but households' indebtedness still rose. I don't think the amortisation requirement will help a lot. The Riksbank's calculations show, as we have seen several times in the committee, that the amortisation requirement only affects new borrowers and therefore has only a small effect in the long term. More measures are therefore needed. The main measure affecting the entire stock of loans would be to reduce the interest rebates. Today, the government is helping to increase debt and thereby create financial instability. When households can afford to borrow more money due to interest rebates, prices of dwellings increase and indebtedness increases. We can't have that. It is necessary for the policy to agree on a reduction in interest rebates as soon as possible, because there is no lack of risk warnings. Interest rebates also have a very doubtful distributional policy profile. They benefit rich people in short, but we're all in on it. At the same time, the interest deduction is a ticked bomb that the Konjunkturinstitutet has warned for can equal SEK 60 billion or more than 1 percent of the bnp in 2024 if nothing is done. That is why we urgently need to introduce a ceiling so that the rich who have extreme interest costs are not subsidised by our welfare. There are two parties in Sweden that have tried to be government-supporting and take responsibility for the state finances, and that is the Social Democrats and the Moderates. Unfortunately, it does very little about household indebtedness more than talking. Minister of Finance Andersson may consider stepping down interest rebates if they had done so at the same time as the moderate-led government of Reinfeldt removed the property tax. It has been a while now, and the debt problems are here and now. But the Moderates are hardly better. During their time in power, the property tax was removed, the property tax was removed, rents were converted into tenant-owners and construction was effectively stopped. There you can talk about throwing more wood on the fire so that household indebtedness would really rise. I think it is time to move from words to deeds and do something about the interest rebate now. Some economists are also advocating the introduction of a debt-to-GDP ceiling. I personally have more doubts about that measure. Today we have a great segregation, especially in our cities. If our income directly without an overall test causes us to be sifted away from certain areas, I believe that it risks further strengthening segregation. Instead, a policy of action is needed to increase the construction of rental apartments. I am therefore pleased that the Left Party, together with the government, has negotiated the biggest housing policy initiative in 20 years. It is a policy that can make a difference and that can mean that as a young person you can get a tenancy to live in instead of debting yourself over your ears to even move away from home. Having said that, Mr President, I am in favour of the draft decision contained in the report.

i-7783f4acbf7a65e2-61	Mr President, I would like to I would like to begin by calling for the committee's proposal in the report to be approved and for the motion to be rejected. The proposal means that the Riksdag now gives its consent to the Riksbank to enter into a credit agreement with the International Monetary Fund, the IMF, of a maximum of 500 million so-called special drawing rights, which corresponds to SEK 5.8 billion. The background is that the IMF has turned to a number of Member States in addition to the 14 Member States which are currently lenders and asked whether we can provide additional loan funds to the Poverty Reduction and Growth Trust (PRGT) to cover low-income countries' needs for loans up to the mid-2020s. The current resources in the loan system are expected to be used in 2016. Mr President, I would like to It is important to know for the members of the Riksdag that the PRGT is financed alongside the IMF's regular lending. It in turn consists of two different parts: one loan part and one interest subsidy component. The subsidy component is self-financed, including through the transfer of part of the profits from the IMF's gold sales in 2009 and 2010 to the Fund. Sweden is a major donor of subsidies to the PRT and has contributed by the Riksbank having transferred our share of the profits from gold sales in autumn 2012 and spring 2013. Sweden has also participated in the subsidy part of the fund through aid funds, by 2010. However, Sweden has not previously contributed to the loan part of the fund. As I said, the subsidy part of the PRGT is expected to be self-financing in the future, which means that the need for loan resources will increase. What suggests that Sweden should now also contribute to the loan part is that Sweden with the Nordic-Baltic constituency has historically been very positive to the IMF's work with the low-income countries. This work has also been successful. We believe that the IMF plays an important role in multilateral development cooperation that no other organisation can fulfil in the same way. Several relatively financially strong countries already contribute to the PRGT loan component, such as Denmark, Belgium, France, Holland, Japan, China, Canada, Norway and Switzerland. The committee believes, however, that it is important for Sweden to participate in the multilateral development work and also notes that the credit risk associated with lending via PRT is very low. It is important for the members of the Riksdag to know that, as a guarantee for the loans, there is currently a risk reserve equivalent to just over 60 per cent of the outstanding loans. Mr President, I would like to Having said that, it is important to note in particular that the committee states that there may be reason to question whether the current lending is entirely within the framework of the Riksbank's core business. As I have said a few times now, participating in and contributing to multilateral development cooperation is very important. The question, however, is, according to the committee, how far the Riksbank's mandate actually extends in this respect. It is therefore reasonable to ask whether it is no more a part of foreign and aid policy than a task for the Central Bank itself. Several other countries have chosen to decide on government guarantees where the central banks provide lending. The committee therefore believes that liability relations and financing of the Swedish IMF's commitments should be seen in a forthcoming review of the Sveriges Riksbank Act. Pending such a review, however, the committee believes that it is important that Sweden, through the Riksbank, contributes to the loan part of the PRT.

The Swedish Democrats have a positive view of free and open trade in goods and services. Trade reduces protectionism and conveys contacts and skills between countries and continents. Global trade is thus a major contributor to both increased prosperity and reduced poverty. We believe that good trade relations and competitively neutral global trade agreements are of paramount importance and that they generate a well-balanced trade policy and contribute to healthy competitive conditions. This is in the interests of: Sweden's industry and its trading partners, which together account for about one fifth of our GDP and three quarters of exports while directly or indirectly employing around 1 million people. Trade is thus the backbone of the Swedish economy. The Swedish Democrats intend to strengthen the competitiveness of Swedish industry through increased trade. Our goal is to increase market shares, growth and profit margins for Swedish companies and that this is done in real terms with comparable countries. However, we see a need for additional factors regarding competition. We therefore believe that there is a need for an overall competition review and hence a review of the so-called competitive factors. At the same time, however, the Swedish Democrats want to emphasise that it is the market's task to review the competitiveness of their own sectors and that policies should not go in and regulate in detail at the minimum possible level, but we see the need for additional factors and an overall competition review regarding larger measurement areas as a whole. An increasing number of products contain parts produced elsewhere in the world, and the chain is to some extent positive, although the desire for geographical proximity to value chains should be taken into account. Swedish companies are allowed to take part in a global market with an increased variety of products. This increases the competitiveness of domestic production and thus also benefits exports. However, we believe that the need for imports needs to be investigated in the long term. Political instability or pressure in exporting nations, monopolistic positions of countries on certain products or natural causes can change the global trade market. The Left Party believes that in the long term all modes of transport should bear their socio-economic costs. Aviation, too, must contribute to emission reductions in order to achieve our climate and environmental objectives. Calculated per passenger-kilometre, aviation is the mode of travel that affects our environment most. Since aviation is not a climate-friendly mode of transport, it should only be used when other alternatives are not available. State involvement in aviation infrastructure is in some cases justified in order to ensure accessibility and regional development throughout the country. It is not justifiable for the state to finance aviation without taking into account our environmental objectives. This means that other modes of transport, not least rail investments, must be given priority in order to improve access to our international airports. We now see how economic doubts have arisen at several regional airports since the state left its responsibilities and allowed municipalities and regions to take over. This has involved more and more creative solutions, on the margins of legislation and regulation. And that has often meant that taxpayers in each municipality or region had black-peeettes when the business community should have direct flights. It is not a responsible policy. Therefore, the export strategy should not promote more direct flights abroad from regional and state airports, but instead prioritise so that it will be easy to get to our international airports in the Nordic region with rail, for example. Several international trade agreements are identified as an important part of Sweden's export strategy. However, several of the agreements risk undermining democratic values. Since the summer of 2013, negotiations have been ongoing between the EU and the US on a joint trade and investment agreement: TTIP. If the agreement were to become a reality, it would be the most extensive of its kind in the world. In parallel with the TTIP negotiations, there are several other current international trade agreements that may ultimately have a significant impact on Sweden. It is the EU's trade agreement with Canada, the CETA agreement, and the multilateral TISA service trade agreement. The common denominator for these international trade agreements is that they have content that can have major negative consequences for the countries covered. If these agreements become a reality, they will fundamentally affect the laws and regulations of the States concerned. All agreements have a clear focus on increased privatisation, liberalisation and weakening of legislation in a number of areas. In the long run, this could mean major deteriorations in areas such as agriculture, food safety, climate, the environment, energy, public services and workers' rights. The only real winners are the big companies, which are given greater opportunities through the agreements to increase their profits. The Left Party considers these agreements to be a fundamental threat to democracy. Deals in trade agreements should not take precedence over states' right to legislate and regulate. Large companies should not be allowed to dictate the conditions under which legislation should prevail in individual countries. That is why we say no to these types of trade agreements. The idea that countries bound together by trade are less likely to end up in armed conflict remains topical. It was a basic idea of the Coal and Steel Union, which was formed after the Second World War and then developed into today's European Union. But throughout history, there are also too many examples of trade that have had devastating consequences. The trade in slaves, where people were abducted from Africa to be sold to North and South America, certainly created economic growth and made some rich. But the vast majority paid a terrible price, and the wounds are not yet healed. This deeply inhumane trade has rightly been thrown into the dump of history. However, we must not forget how important it is for both states and companies to assume their responsibilities for trade and exports to be conducted on the basis of values based on respect for human rights, the environment and democracy. This is well expressed in the agreement that S and MP made when we formed a government: are the world's countries increasingly economically dependent on each other and share responsibility for the environment on our planet. The benefits of globalisation will be shared by all. It requires people to be put ahead of the market. Democracy needs to be globalised and globalisation democratised. Greater efforts must be made to increase the number of democratic states in the world. ..................................................... We also have the Swedish policy for global development, PGU. It means that all policy decisions and policies should take into account human rights, democratic participation of people, the fight against poverty and environmental sustainability. It was decided by a unanimous parliament in 2003. The government takes it very seriously as well as the new development goals adopted by the UN, Agenda 2030, where trade forms an important part of sustainable development. There is much to be said about the export strategy and about the action plan on sustainable entrepreneurship, which we are also going to debate in this House today. There are many steps in the right direction towards good trade and exports. I can't talk about everything. I'll just have to dwell on a few things. Companies, large and small in different industries, act as never before across borders. However, the market does not consist solely of private producers and consumers. States have always played an important role in trade, often as an independent player, but also by creating rules on how trade should be conducted and on what terms and conditions. That is, of course, still the case today. The state can and should support the export of goods and services that help solve the major global societal challenges. There are many wise measures in the Government's export strategy that can help to seize the enormous potential of Swedish companies in helping to solve, for example, environmental challenges. Coordination and support for companies so that several companies can join forces and take on major projects such as renewable energy, waste management and community planning is important and good. It can provide real win-win solutions. The regional export centres, which have already begun to open, are very important to harness the knowledge and localities that exist throughout the country. We must not forget the municipal companies. They have a lot to contribute, not least in the field of the environment and in the area of urban planning. There are, moreover, further obstacles to be removed. Although there is very good in this strategy, there are more things to do. In fact, municipal companies are limited by outdated legislation which needs to be modernised so that they can compete and export on an equal footing with other companies. The world can't afford not to take advantage of all the good powers. Another good thing in the export strategy is that we will establish more consulates and honorary consulates in the world. As has been mentioned, there are many Swedes with backgrounds in other countries. They have unique opportunities to make great efforts to benefit both Sweden and Swedish export companies as well as their former home countries. I spoke some time ago with representatives of the Erbil Chamber of Commerce in Iraqi Kurdistan. It calls for cooperation with Sweden in order to move forward on renewable energy and to break oil dependence, develop organic farming and help young people and citizens in general to create businesses, make a living at home and avoid leaving abroad. It is an example of the need for Swedish representation in more parts of the world. The tourism industry has been mentioned, and I would like to take this opportunity to underline the importance of this growing industry. Helena Lindahl gave us figures on how many jobs and how much money it generates. Then it is important that we take good care of the unique natural and cultural environments that tourists from near and far want to come here and experience. In all complex contexts, conflicts between different interests arise. Then it is important to keep your head cool and remember that, when it comes to the exploitation of natural resources, minerals and so on, it can often be more economically profitable – not just ecologically – to leave the old forest here and where and to leave minerals lying sometimes, both for society as well as for companies and jobs in the long term. There has been much talk about flights here, but how nice would it be with night trains from the continent – what holidays could it not be? Mr President, I would like to After all, increased trade is not automatically beneficial at all times. I want to raise my eyes a little bit to get perspective. It depends on what is sold and on what conditions. For example, the world hardly needs more arms trade. On the other hand, the need for solar cells, malaria medicine and the like is enormous. It is important to keep in mind what the great environmental economist Herman Daly pointed out, namely that it is more sensible to change recipes than to send cookies between countries. The latter generates unnecessary transport which creates unnecessary environmental burdens, and the economic growth that arises is therefore, in Dalys's words, uneconomically overall. Responsible exports and trade require a regulatory framework that protects people and the environment and does not unilaterally favour large voiced transnational companies at the expense of other interests. In many trade agreements there are so-called investor state defence settlements, mechanisms that give companies the right to sue states if they believe that states have restricted their ability to make profits or their investments. I would like to take this opportunity to highlight something that may not be mentioned as often, namely that people affected by business activities must also be able to assert their rights and receive compensation and redress. Although the intentions are the best, human rights can sometimes go wrong and human rights can be violated or the environment destroyed. Nature and future generations must also have a voice, and there must be a sort of equality before the law for people and the environment as well as for businesses. There are several possible ways that I would like to develop in my next speech, if I have time. But let me briefly mention here that there is, among other things, a proposal for international legislation on ecocid, that is, serious destruction of nature and habitat. This is one thing that Sweden could well do in the UN. Indeed, it is a major step forward that the Government in the Sustainable Business Action Plan wants to support and encourage companies to do right and respect human rights through voluntary initiatives, regulations and guidelines, in order to protect human rights and the environment. However, I think that in a next step we need to consider whether to move forward and work towards binding rules for large transnational companies. It is a work that was carried out in the UN but which was closed down in the 1990s. Now it has been mentioned again in a working group, and I think that Sweden could do a great deal of good. Having said all that, I support the proposal in the report and reject the motions.

i-7783f4acbf7a65e2-286	Mr President, I would like to I would like to start this debate by actually thanking the government for the praise it has received from many parties for developing this export strategy. I am somewhat biased, but I have to say that this issue is among the most important issues for us to discuss in this House because the Swedish economy is extremely dependent on our exports and on our international trade relations. And this debate testifies to that. I would like to start by mentioning that today's Industry on 29 February says: Swedish exports are gaining ground. Many in the Committee on Economic Affairs of course read Dagens Industri at the time. But it is the first time in 20 years that Swedish exports gain market share in international markets. This is a very gratifying development because a background to this export strategy is, of course, the importance of exports for our Swedish economy. But there is also a historical perspective, that we now have a number of years behind us where Swedish exports have lost ground. If you look at some of our competitor countries, such as Germany and the Netherlands, their exports have developed almost twice as well as Swedish exports over a number of years. This, of course, is not sustainable in the long term. Very much of the Swedish economy is based on exports and trade with other countries and contributes a lot to our bnp but of course also to the financing of our welfare. I think that the example of Västra Götaland and the lifting that is now taking place in exports there demonstrate the enormous importance that exports have for employment in our country. When exports are involved in drawing our economy as it is now, we also see positive developments in the Swedish labour market – more new jobs but also falling unemployment. This therefore means a great deal to the Swedish economy. It is clear that all of us who are taking part in this debate today share the understanding of the importance of exports and trade for Sweden. However, I would like to mention just one figure to bring this to light. At the end of last year, the National Board of Trade presented a report showing that the direct export industry currently employs 678 000 people. However, if we include those companies that are directly dependent on exports for their activities and support the export sector, 1.3 million people go to work every day thanks to Swedish exports. This is therefore incredibly important for the Swedish economy. In principle, every third person who goes to work does so thanks to Swedish exports. This is part of the background to the government's choice – it was almost the first thing we did in government – to prioritise the work on exports, develop this export strategy and also change up export support work rather dramatically in budgetary terms. When we look at the problem pictures that still exist of Swedish exports, we are most dependent on exports to our close neighbours. It's no wonder. About 70 per cent of Swedish exports go to Europe. However, as we look forward, we see that around 80% of the expected growth in the world economy will take place outside Europe. Therefore, we need to work to reach more growth markets around the world so that Sweden does not miss out on the export development that we have the opportunity to take advantage of for the Swedish economy. Another thing that I think is very important and that is behind this strategy is that only 10% of Swedish companies export. There is a huge potential to get more SMEs to dare and want to take the step into the export market. We know that it is very important for the Swedish economy, but it is also very important for employment in our country. It is through small and medium-sized enterprises, which are taking the step into the export market and need to expand and make new jobs, that we can have a major employment effect. The strategy actually identifies a number of challenges. More small and medium-sized enterprises must dare and want to export, as I have just mentioned. Swedish goods, services and systems must reach higher up the processing chain. Sweden's attractiveness for investment, skills and tourism must increase. And global trade must be kept open. This is the basis of the government's export strategy. In the export strategy, there are 22 concrete actions identified that we are now working on to make it a reality. One can say that there is a focus not only on getting small and medium-sized enterprises to take the step but also, which is important, on not forgetting our large export companies in Sweden and their opportunities to win not least the big business in a global arena. This means a great deal to the Swedish economy and long-term to our prosperity. The development of the strategy has taken place in very close dialogue with the business community. Both individual companies and trade unions as well as trade unions have contributed to the work of thinking about what needs to be done further in order to improve our export promotion. I would like to thank you for that dialogue. The reception received by the export strategy, not least by industry, shows that it believes that we have followed up the talks well and identified important areas that they have pointed out and that we are now working on. This applies both to large companies and to small and medium-sized enterprises. Work is now under way to implement and implement the 22 initiatives. I would like to give some examples to Parliament to show how the work is going on and what is happening in the strategy. The first is that we are working very hard to make government export promotion more effective. It was ironized a little bit about the concept of Team Sweden and the attempt to get the Swedish actors together and cooperate a lot. I will say that in the dialogues we had it was an issue that involved business more than anyone else. They said: There are too many actors, it is hard to know who does what. Can you not coordinate efforts to make it easier for companies to find the right support that still exists and develop the support available for SMEs to go on export? That is why we are creating an organisation here at home to try to create better and clearer entrances for companies around Sweden, through the regional export centres we have now decided on – the first six – to ensure that companies get a door in but also help within 24 hours. It is also a coordination of our efforts in important international markets. The fact that all our players can be helped and pulled in the same direction means a lot for us to succeed not least in more difficult markets around the world. After the summer we will start the digital export information, a single window we are collecting on the website verkat.se, to show the state support available to get advice, marketing, financing etc. in a single place. Business Sweden is now also in the starting pits to launch an information campaign aimed at SMEs to inform about the importance of exports and what support and help you can get to internationalise your business. The strategic work to win more of the largest global business has begun, and Business Sweden, together with the Confederation of Swedish Enterprise, has already identified a number of pilot projects to see how to work strategically to win the major international business. Several efforts have been undertaken to strengthen Sweden's image. Recently, for example, a digital display window for cultural and creative industries was launched. It was launched in Austin, USA, and we make special efforts with the Swedish image in India, for example. We are also making special efforts to increase tourism promotion in both India and China; in the spring ten new visa offices will be opened in China to facilitate, for example, tourism flows to Sweden. The UN strategy for winning major international procurements has also moved on. We have made a resource enhancement of our presence in New York to be able to work strategically in order for Sweden to have the opportunity to take home some of the major business that exists in the UN system. We also work to strengthen Swedish talent through, for example, the Swedish Institute. We notice that we are on the move with efforts. In many of these 22 areas, work is progressing. I wanted to take some time to show that there is now a full focus on implementation. There is also a great deal of transparency – we notice that something is not working properly we are doing if and doing the right thing, because the ambition is to increase our success in international markets. Then we shall also learn from what we do in the meantime. As some of you have highlighted in the debate, I would also like to emphasise the importance of implementing the export strategy in line with the National Action Plan on Business and Human Rights. This is the first time a government has linked these two issues together and is working strategically on it. This is all the more important because a number of the priority markets we point out in the export strategy are difficult markets where there is widespread corruption or a lack of human rights. Therefore, it is even more important to work with sustainable entrepreneurship together with the issue of export strategy, and we think that is very important. We see that the export strategy will contribute to the government's overall goal of combating unemployment and achieving the EU's lowest unemployment rate by 2020. We are in the process of implementation, and I would like to conclude by saying that this involves a great deal of change, but hopefully also a long-term commitment on the part of Sweden to take export and trade issues more seriously. I also hope that in the future we can achieve greater political agreement on the importance of these issues. The debate that has so far been has reflected a great deal of agreement on the importance, but it is also important that we reach agreement not least on the future financial effort to do so. I therefore hope that we will see more positive headlines on Swedish exports in the coming years. At least that's what we're working on, and hopefully the export strategy will lead to that.

They may make sports shoes of different kinds and tell us about sustainability factors, just as we are talking about food and what they contain. It gives a picture of what it's all about. This is where companies connect via specific codes, accounting models, accounting standards and the like in the CSR area. If you want, you can do it in different ways to solve the same task. Detailed regulation of companies does not promote Swedish growth and development for Swedish companies. That was the whole of the previous debate. In addition to politics, there are companies that are far better than we politicians can influence, and they affect people to a large extent. One example is Puma, which uses representatives around the world who use their products and thus affect people in different ways. Mr President, I would like to Entrepreneurship is central to a globally small export-dependent country like Sweden and to get the wheels to spin for growth in localities out into regions and further nationally and internationally. Here, from the tax side, we can address BEPS, base erosion and profit shifting, within the OECD. Within the state-owned companies, the alliance government has developed sustainability criteria, and the current government is continuing to work on them. It is good when nations across borders can find wise solutions for development. I would much rather talk about smart solutions than imposing charges. We have everything to gain from such solutions. The values expressed in the UN principles and the OECD Guidelines for Multinational Enterprises assume that Swedish companies respect human rights and basic environmental standards when acting in foreign markets. We believe that parts of the government's policy will hit the business community hard because of the way it chooses to work with sustainability, not least against small businesses. When you look at an industry such as construction, you can see that the government now places higher demands on Swedish companies than on companies that will compete from other countries. I do not believe in a model that claims to stand for sustainability but dismantles Swedish companies' opportunities for the benefit of others. It is not sustainable for Sweden. The idea is not and cannot be to discriminate against Swedish companies over companies from other countries. It is not sustainable that it can be more beneficial for a construction agency from Poland than one from Sweden to operate here. We believe that Sweden's challenge instead lies in ensuring energy supply and maintaining the desire for long-term energy investments. Such investments must, of course, be made in parallel with long-term good environmental development in order not to jeopardise Sweden's or Europe's global competitiveness. If excessive costs are imposed on companies, this will hamper economic growth and thus reduce the resources for research and development. Of course, companies join voluntarily. They are registered, and you can also have your company certified. This is also where society comes in, the interaction between the public and the private. Against this background, the committee considered that the government should examine whether the management chosen by Denmark is compatible with EU rules in this area. At the same time, I can state that, according to the directive for the investigation, the Swedish Transport Agency will report on this work by 1 May 2017 – an investigation period of one and a half years. I believe that the specified investigation period is too long because it was apparent from the evaluation presented by the Swedish Transport Agency in March 2015 that there are already examples of other countries that have chosen other systems that are more flexible than the Swedish one in that smaller deviations from the driving and rest time rules are allowed before the penalty fee is due. I believe that the investigation period for the Swedish Transport Agency's mission is, to say the least, extended and that the investigation period should therefore be shortened with the aim of introducing the possibility of minor deviations from driving and rest times by 2016. i-7783f4acbf7a65e2-637	Mr President, I would like to In recent years, Swedish truck traffic has been severely exposed to international competition. Current rules provide that only occasional transport is permitted and allow a foreign carrier who has completed an international carriage of goods to carry out a maximum of three domestic transport operations in another EU country for seven days. This regulatory framework is quite difficult to get to work because it is quite easy to slip away from it and abuse it. Competition is intensifying and the market share of foreign lorries on the roads is increasing. But rentals look the same all over Sweden. If you enter a rental apartment from the 1990s, it looks the same whether it is an apartment in Malmö, Stockholm or Östersund. You recognize the tiles, the bathtub and the taps. Today we have standardised housing for rentals. This is not the way forward. One possibility is that tenants may make different choices and selections. This would promote the right to rent as a form of housing. The fact that you can adapt your accommodation to your own personal choices takes advantage of the potential of the tenure form. Instead of standardisation, a mixture can allow for a lower standard for those who so desire and perhaps even for a higher standard for those who want it. For example, there are young people who find it difficult to get enough money, whose housing horizon may be a little shorter and who think that as low a rent as possible is central. At the same time, it should be possible to build even more expensive for those who want high service and high standards of material selection. The variety prepares the way to make rental rights more attractive and provide a wider range of rentals. Changing and developing rental law leads to social engagement and opportunities to develop peripheral services and increase quality of housing. Ownership apartments have existed since 2009 but only in new production. In other countries, ownership is often the most common form of housing. Allowing a variety of living forms counteracts housing segregation and gives people the opportunity to invest in their accommodation without having to move. In order to further simplify this transition, the majority of the Committee on Civil Liberties also wants the government to develop a form of housing rental system. Through the owner-occupied apartment reform, the housing market has added another form of housing for residential blocks of flats. This contributes to the diversity of housing and increases the freedom of choice of those who are able to finance the acquisition of an owner's apartment. So far, relatively few ownership apartments have been created, which is linked to the economic situation and some uncertainty among market players about the new property law. In the housing sector, however, there is confidence in the owner-occupied apartment as a form of housing, and more and more new production is planned. Another factor that has limited the impact of the ownership apartment reform is that owner apartments have been created only in connection with new production. The possibility of converting existing residential units into owner-occupied dwellings would contribute to the creation of owner-occupied apartments, with the positive effects associated with this. Another aspect that I would like to mention is that of rental statistics. In order to strengthen consumers' power and knowledge of rental development, we need to strengthen how rental statistics are presented. The statistics need to be made available to residential consumers. Good rental statistics would improve the debate on rental rights and clarify the rules. Then we get black and white information about price differences, for example between a rental right in a suburban area and one at an exclusive inner city address. If the price difference is non-existent, something that is repeatedly indicated, it leads to a number of questions about the reasonableness of this. Mr President, I would like to The Center Party does not want to introduce market rents through a single political decision, but we want to see a development where it is supply and demand that sets rental levels and where the power and choice of tenants are strengthened by the fact that property owners compete with each other to meet their demand. It may take time, and until we have the necessary housing facilities, there may be gradual changes along these lines, with the aim that within 20 years we will have a free market for rental rights, just as we already have for other forms of leasing. Then, just as now, there is property protection and protection against unfair renting and sudden unjustified increases in rent. I will now explain how we want this to happen. Firstly, we want to introduce open rental statistics that facilitate comparisons between rents, management quality, housing standards and location. Here there is a joint reservation from us in the Alliance, and of course I support it. Secondly, we want to allow new production rents, so-called presumption rents, to affect the rent level also for older rentals in the same area. With too large differences between equivalent housing in the same area, mobility is reduced. The step into the utility value system becomes too large after 10 or 15 years, when the presumption rent expires. Thirdly, we want to change the system of presumption rents. They are an emergency solution that must remain, but we should know that only one third of the new rents are set with the support of these. The Tenant's Association should not, as it is today, be able to refuse to negotiate presuming rents. When private property owners are excluded from negotiation, they must set the rent themselves a, with the risk that it will be reconsidered and become unprofitable. These things go Centerpar tiet into its reservation number 5. Fourthly, we want to allow owner-occupied apartments also in existing portfolios of rental and tenant-ownership rights, facilitate housing saving for young people and offer the possibility of entering into lease agreements where you can subsequently pay off and buy off your home, as is common in France. Here is an announcement to the government, as mentioned earlier. We note that the government, with its support party Left, would rather see a lack of housing than people being given the opportunity to own their housing. Fifthly, we then want to introduce a system of free contract law on newly produced rentals, in which the parties themselves can agree on contractual terms and monthly rentals. These rents will then be tested for reasonableness, as is the case today, and increases in rents will follow the consumer price index. To then allow free contract law means that we get a rental right that gives the tenant a real influence and an opportunity to shape the apartment and the contract terms according to their own wishes in consultation with the housing company. Sixthly, we want to maintain possession protection and protection against sudden and unjustified increases, strengthen the tenant's position by means of own choice and by applying individual measurement of water, electricity and heat where it is economically and technically feasible. Here we have the second alliance-common reservation that I support. Mr President, I would like to With the gradual introduction of these reforms, freedom of contract can be introduced when moving into vacant apartments. Only then, when there is a full balance between supply and demand, can there be free renting also for existing and current rental contracts by means of an inception that can take place for a very long time, five to ten years if necessary. The tenancy is still based on the principle that everyone should have the right to live in a rental apartment, even in the most attractive locations. As a result, in many places we have use-value rents that are significantly lower than the residents are prepared to pay. It keeps private debt down. A professional landlord manages real estate management in a hopefully good and rational way. The right to rent can be mediated in a way that counteracts segregation – unlike the right-of-occupancy property and the villa, where it is the thickness of the wallet that determines. In order for the rental right to remain a long-term housing form for a large part of the population, we see a need to strengthen the financing of the construction. The funding we have today puts rental rights at a disadvantage. We want to ensure that the State takes over part of the risk of the rental right's financing by offering top loans replacing most of the capital that the private or public-interest investor itself needs to bring in. In this way, many more, including the economically weaker builders, can enter the market and build. If the state does not do this, there is already a clear limit to how much we can build. The use of land for speculative purposes must be countered. We see that land allocated to rental projects is built up faster, and the rental right is a form of lease that all people have access to. We therefore want to reward municipalities that plan land for rental construction. We want to see that all districts in all parts of Sweden should be able to have different types of housing, i.e. rentals in Askim and tenant-owners in Angered. Ownership rights would increase freedom of choice, not least in the million-programme areas where there is often no other than rental rights available. You should also be able to make a stay in your own area. But the Liberals want to go further; we do not think that is enough. It shall also be possible to convert right-of-occupancy apartments and detached homes into property rights. It is not possible today, nor will it be with this announcement. That's why we have a reservation about it. We also see clearly that the government parties, the Left Party and the Swedish Democrats do not in any way have a policy that meets the challenges we have in the Swedish housing market. There is a lot of talk about solidarity and everyone's right to housing, but in practice they are defending a system that has led to 350 000 young adults lacking their own housing. They defend a system where 700 000 homes are missing and where you have to queue longer to get a leasehold than East Germans needed to queue up to get a Trabant car in East Germany. The Left Party, the Social Democrats, the Environmental Party and the Swedish Democrats are thus defending this system. I think you have a responsibility to dare change a system that does not work. What does this lead to? Yes, to social segregation and exclusion. This is the result of the Swedish model that the Social Democrats defend. I marvel at the reluctance to see reality as it is. None of the measures highlighted by the Liberals will make society more segregated. Liberals take the challenge seriously with wise, necessary and sometimes difficult balances. Unlike the parties I have just mentioned, we want to see long-term and radical reforms to solve the Swedish housing crisis. I would like to mention four points in particular. First of all, we want to see free renting in new construction. The Swedish rent regulation is described by the OECD as one of the strictest among their member countries. We therefore also differ from our Nordic neighbours. In Norway, Finland, Denmark and Iceland there are market rents in parts of or the entire rental market. I can guarantee that it does not mean the doomsday that Nooshi Dadgostar paints. I can guarantee that people do not go around homeless people in Oslo, Copenhagen, Helsinki and Reykjavik. When the Left Party shouts wolf, it is hypocrisy. Make study visits in Oslo, Copenhagen and He lsingfors and see if people are homeless there! They're not. On the other hand, they have a better functioning housing market; it is easier to get housing there. It is in practice easy to get a place to live in Oslo or Copenhagen. I do not understand the opposition to this when practice shows the opposite. The criticism of Swedish rental regulation comes from the European Commission, the OECD, the IMF, the World Bank and the Council of Finance Policy. All of these argue that the current system does not work. It is in the light of these expert statements, and not the views of the Left Party, that we propose free leasing in new production by allowing the rent under the presumption rent system to also be freely agreed between the lessor and the lessor. Now Nooshi Dadgostar will probably take a line on me and mention the report Ramböll produced on behalf of the Tenant Association. The problem is that what Nooshi Dadgostar always compares to is not at all the proposal put forward by the Liberals. We want to see a presumption rent system that applies to everyone without a shorter time limit. It should be combined with a strong possession protection and a predictable indexation throughout the period the tenant chooses to stay. This differs considerably from the doomsday proposals that Nooshi Dadgostar paints. In addition, the latest EU survey shows that the production of rental apartments would increase by 6% with the Liberals' proposal. Our proposal would therefore give more rents, I can inform the parties on the left. Secondly, we want to see reforms in the utility value system for increased mobility. Even within the existing stock, renting should in future reflect more consumer demand and individual choices. Within the utility value system, location and choice need to have a greater impact so that rental levels more closely reflect supply and demand. Nobody's moving because they want parquet floors. The situation and public transport are crucial. A person growing up in Angered or in Bishopsgården does not have the same security, school results, outdoor environment, access to services and services as a person living in Majorna or Askim. It is hardly possible to defend the fact that the rents in Angered are as high as those in Majorna. There is no justification for the fact that the rents in Bishopsgården are as high as those in Askim. That's completely unreasonable. In the future, rents must reflect demand much better. Thirdly, we want to see better opportunities for second-hand rental. There is no party in the Swedish Parliament that really believes that we will manage to build 700 000 apartments until 2025. We must therefore make better use of the existing stock. Johan Löfstrand, who is the spokesperson of the Social Democrats for housing policy, has himself acknowledged this in a conversation with the Rights of Housing where I was present. Liberals therefore want to see more free renting when renting villas, flats and owner-occupied apartments. Since 2013, there have been freer rules, and this has led to more advertised second-hand housing in owned dwellings. We now propose a further step with a more free renting when renting villas, flats and owner-occupied apartments through a more generous calculation of reasonable rent. We also believe that the ceiling for tax-free rental income should be increased so that more people dare to report white. Fourthly, we want a rental statistics in Sweden. The power of tenants as consumers must be strengthened. Knowledge gives power, and knowledge about the price of renting a home is particularly important because housing costs make up a large part of a household's expenses. It is therefore essential that reliable information is available. We therefore believe that public rental statistics should be developed so that a transparent system can be established. Our reservations mention many other things that we would like to change, but I choose to stop here.

Firstly, the 10th report on relations between the European Commission and the national parliaments, the so-called liaison report. The KU is unanimous in this opinion, but it is nevertheless important to highlight the opinion in this House. I would like to touch on certain aspects that I find of particular interest. With regard to the communication report, relations between the Commission and national parliaments were strengthened when the Commission presented the so-called Barroso initiative in 2006, also known as the political dialogue. It aims to increase the involvement of parliaments in European affairs. The report contains, inter alia, an account of the subjects raised by national Parliaments to the Commission in the context of the political dialogue. In the report, the Commission notes in 2014 a significant reduction in the number of opinions and reasoned opinions compared to the previous year. The decrease corresponds to approximately 19%. However, this decrease was mainly due to fewer initiatives being presented during the year due to the change of Commission. It is also clear that 80 per cent of the opinions came from the ten most active parliaments, including the Swedish Parliament. This shows that the activity of the political dialogue is unevenly divided between the chambers of national parliaments. I think it is important to develop the interparliamentary exchange of information. It would not only ensure the effective exercise of parliamentary powers but also contribute to a more balanced distribution of the participation of national parliaments in the framework of political dialogue. Today, there are opportunities for collaboration through, for example, Cosac or IPEX, but these forums have not been fully sufficient to achieve a meaningful exchange of information. As in previous years, the political dialogue of national parliaments with the Commission mainly concerned legislative proposals. The Commission encourages national parliaments to participate in the preparatory legislative phase, either by responding to public consultations or by commenting on strategic documents such as Green Papers. To date, however, very few Parliaments have expressed their views before a legislative proposal has been adopted. In any case, the Committee on Constitutional Affairs welcomes the fact that national parliaments are involved in examining and reviewing Union documents that precede the legislative process. The committee also believes that the Commission's decision to forward new legislative proposals and consultation documents directly to national Parliaments, within the framework of the Barroso initiative, can contribute to an in-depth debate on Union cooperation within the Member States. With regard to the subsidiarity report, the Commission sets out its work on assessing and justifying proposals based on the principles of subsidiarity and proportionality. This report underlines that respect for the principles of subsidiarity and proportionality will be central to the work of the new Commission, which should be seen as a positive development. The Commission also refers to the Better Regulation Package adopted in May 2015 which provides, inter alia, updated guidance on the assessment of the principles in the context of impact assessments of new initiatives. In this context, the committee has noted that for several years, most recently in the Communication "Better Regulation for Better Results – An EU Agenda", the Commission has stressed that each proposal should be accompanied by a better justification. Nevertheless, we were able to note that more than 15% of the legislative proposals sent to national parliaments for subsidiarity review were completely lacking. The motivations were also often brief. We, the Constitutional Committee, have on several occasions called for detailed justifications in connection with the Commission's transmission of legislative proposals to the national parliaments. It is certainly appropriate in this opinion to recall once again that Protocol No 2 obliges the Commission and other proposers to provide justifications based on the principles of subsidiarity and proportionality. The absence of justification clearly infringes the Protocol and, in turn, makes it difficult for national Parliaments to fulfil their Treaty obligations under the Protocol. In the opinion of the Constitutional Committee, this represents a weakness in the legislative process within the Union, for which we believe the Commission is ultimately responsible. Mr President, I would like to The opinion also states that the Commission's proposal for the establishment of a European Public Prosecutor's Office de facto triggered a yellow card when 13 reasoned opinions were received from national Parliaments on the draft law. Nevertheless, the Commission maintained its legislative proposal, which has led some national parliaments to submit opinions to the Commission in 2014 on this issue. In these opinions, Parliaments expressed dissatisfaction with the Commission's decision to stick to its proposal. The Commission was also criticised for not responding to the reasoned opinions until after it had decided to stick to its proposal. My view in this part is that criticism has largely been justified in this particular case. The yellow card procedure is a political tool which the national parliaments have the opportunity to make use of and also did in the above-mentioned case. In this respect, the Commission's dialogue should reasonably have been better before the Commission finally took its decision. It is also important that the Commission takes into account the views of the Member States in these respects, not least in the further legislative work on the Commission's proposal to establish a European Public Prosecutor's Office, given that the proposal reached the yellow card limit. I would add that it is important from a democratic point of view to do so, but also in view of the European Union's efforts to achieve more effective cooperation. In addition, the opinion presents an initiative by some national parliaments on the so-called green card which is intended to give Parliament greater influence in the legislative process. In addition, the Commission's stated ambition to be more active in its relations with national parliaments is evident. In this connection, the Constitutional Committee recalls that the political dialogue between the Commission and Sweden takes place through the government, which is responsible to the Riksdag. Only within the framework of the subsidiarity control mechanism can Parliament communicate directly with the Commission on the basis of the Treaties. It is therefore important that Parliament, such as our Parliament, should not be disadvantaged because, for constitutional reasons, we do not have the opportunity to participate in the political dialogue. Finally, Mr President, I would just like to mention that the Constitutional Committee points out that several parliamentary committees have pointed out that the Commission is increasingly proposing regulations instead of directives. The Committee on Constitutional Affairs would like to draw the Commission's attention here to the fact that this reduces the scope for national parliaments to adapt legislation at European level to the different constitutional conditions in each individual Member State. In other words, instead of regulations, directives would be better. In the light of the speech, Mr President, I support the position of the Constitutional Committee and urge the Parliament to put the opinion to the vote. I would also like to wish the President, the other Members and everyone at home a happy Easter.

It may seem a little strange given that the gap in working life is growing between the natives and the foreigns. It has been shown that only 25% of foreign-born people have a full-time job after ten years. It may take up to ten years before half of this group is in work, which is far too long. There are far too many obstacles along the way. There is a rigidity and an attitude we need to change both in employers and in unions. Employers are usually afraid of those who are a little different, and unions only look after those who have jobs – their members – and not those who are without jobs. More employers must be encouraged to dare to try someone new or inexperienced. We believe that this could be a road to increased labour force participation. Likewise, an extended probationary period, to twelve months, can be another opportunity for many to really show their worth. During our reign, we introduced a general fixed term of two years. It has become a successful gateway for many new and young people into the labour market. We introduced new start jobs, which have become a really big success, but also entry-level jobs for new arrivals. It is a very advantageous form of employment. We are always prepared to rethink and renew our policy of opening up to more people to get a real job. The present government should also be. Another real issue is the conditions of foreign workers and companies on the Swedish labour market. We all remember the Laval case and all the tours we've had with posting teams, lex Laval, investigations and other things. For greater mobility of both goods and services and better competition for Swedish customers, we do not want to build more barriers or make them higher, and we do not want to increase the requirements for those who come here. It is important to stress that everyone at a Swedish workplace should of course have the right conditions. However, the demands that are now being raised are in danger of building new barriers. It should be safe and secure, but we should not impair intra-EU mobility in terms of the workforce. The fact is that there are already good working conditions for those who come to Sweden and work within the current legislation. The Postings Act has rules that ensure the extra protection needed for those posted, in addition to the protection they actually bring with them from home. The concerns about social dumping and social tourism have also proved to be completely wrong. If you want to improve posted workers' conditions in Sweden, it is therefore much better to actually tighten up the application and control of current laws and regulations. The new rules of the so-called Enforcement Directive have barely come into force yet, and yet the government wants to open up to the amendment of the Posting of Workers Directive. We're against that. For example, many hundreds of thousands of new homes have to be built in Sweden, but there are actually not enough construction workers here. There is hardly a single available construction worker to find in Sweden. So it is necessary that both companies and workers from other countries come to us in order for us to do this. Unfortunately, we mean, the government goes completely LO's business. LO wants to keep the foreign labour force quite far away from Sweden, in order to reduce competition and not threaten the good salary image Swedish construction workers have in an international comparison. We believe that increased mobility for both workers and entrepreneurs creates opportunities for everyone, both for those who come here and for Swedish growth and development. Wage work is of fundamental importance to most people's everyday lives and well-being. That's how you can get a lot of dynamism and a lot of security at the same time. Contractual solutions can adapt the conditions to a reality that is both multifaceted and changing. At the same time, the law sets the framework within which collective agreements are signed, and when reality changes, the law must also keep up – partly because there are problems that should not be agreed upon, partly because, not least after Sweden's accession to the EU, there are laws that make it difficult to sign reasonable collective agreements. What we are seeing today is that these two motives have fallen into shadow. Instead, renting is used to cover staff needs that are in principle permanent. This is not a good development. Temporary workers are in a vulnerable position, find it more difficult to be part of the work community and are less willing to make demands on their work environment. At the same time, threats of replacing permanent staff with temporary staff can be used as a means of power in the employer's hands. That is why we believe that there is a need for clearer regulation of staffing activities. We have a number of concrete proposals on this. The use of rentals should not be allowed to meet permanent needs. It is true that trade unions are trying to arrange this in different ways, but it should not be something for which you have to sell pay space. This is about the basic structure of our labour market. We also want to see a strengthening of trade union influence on the issue of renting-in. Among other things, the absence of collective agreements in itself should be sufficient for trade union veto rights against renting. We do not want to impose the Swedish model on everyone else, but we want to be able to retain our labour market model. Building hasn't wanted to do that for a very long time. It's caused a problem. Then we have said that we are in favour of trying to do this implementing directive. It should be applied properly, but it has not even got to work. And now you want to open up to further changes. As I said, we are afraid that it will rather put away the legs of the Swedish model.
