i-7783f4acbf7a65e2-1483	Mr President, I would like to As the last speaker on this relatively late evening in the House of Parliament, I wanted to talk for a moment about the Employment Committee's report No. 7, which deals with labour law. If there is still anyone in this country who does not believe that there are differences between the parties in the Swedish Parliament, I say: Obey now! In this matter, there is a deep and sharp dividing line in Swedish politics. It's a question about your everyday life. It is about human vision, life value and decency. It is about who should have fundamental rights and who should not have them. It is about our rights in working life – labour law. When we talk about labour law and employment protection, there are always a number of bourgeois politicians who literally see red. For them, employment protection is an obstacle for entrepreneurs to choose their staff. That's kind of how you put it. But in those statements there is no real truth. For a social democrat, job security and a strong labour law are the very foundations of what makes working life, and therefore society, decent. Job security, which is governed by the Employment Protection Act, is what prevents arbitrariness from taking place in the labour market. The bourgeois rhetoric usually consists of the rule of last in and first out meaning that the employer is obliged to terminate those who are last employed regardless of what skills you need at the company – we have heard this evening too. That, of course, is not true. It becomes very clear that it is not true if you go to, for example, the Labour Court and look at how few cases it is that have to do with the rules of rotation. How the order lists are designed is something that trade unions and employers negotiate about. As a former trade union negotiator, I myself have been in many dismissal negotiations. I know that there is a great willingness on the part of the workers' organisations to solve the supply of skills in the workplace and that, for the most part, negotiations with employers are very flexible. Why, then, is it so important to have rules of order at all, can a friend of order ask himself if things always work out? Well, the reason we have the rules is because we want to prevent arbitrariness. Every time you have to resign someone, you have to call a union hearing if you are an employer. This is in order for all workers to have the right to have their case brought before them. You should not be able to get laid off and go out in the event of a lack of work because you are the employee who has asked questions about the work environment or because you have demanded the equipment you are entitled to. You should not end up on the employer's own first-out list because you have children who are often ill, because you are pregnant, because you are hunters, vegans, redheads or because you are not always an avid yes-sayer. Believe me, I have met many good and serious employers during my trade union years, but I also have many rogue employers who have tried to get rid of people on very loose grounds. In that case, the Employment Protection Act and the Rules of Procedure are regulated in the only protection you have as an employee. A working life without that law and without the rules of rotation would create jobs where silence spreads, where no one dares to question anything or even come up with new ideas. You may not even dare to join the union, for you are afraid of losing your job. A form of serfness – we might call it – would spread, and all power would lie with the employer. It is precisely to counter serfness and create a balance in power in the labour market that we in Sweden have developed the Swedish model. We have strong legislation in essence, and we have collective agreements between the parties that can regulate that legislation. The law applies to everyone, whether there are collective agreements in the workplace or not. This is the base. That is labour law. It is basically about which society we want to live in. We want strong individuals who can claim their rights without being fired. Or do we perhaps want to go back 50 years in development, where workers were entirely in the hands of the employer without any rights at all? Then you stood with your hat in hand outside the factory every morning and prayed on your knees to get in and work one day to survive and to support your family. I don't want it that way. Unfortunately, labour law has been relaxed in recent times – for quite a long time, I would say. We have employees who are basically holding the hat in hand, but today it is a mobile phone you are holding, waiting for a text message every morning from an employer to find out if there is a job for a few hours. General fixed-term contracts increase job insecurity. That is something that the government took the first step a few weeks ago to improve, but much remains to be done. We have long probationary contracts today, just as the Left Party's Ali Esbati has just mentioned, compared with what it was like before. It is a very insecure form of work, as you are completely left to the employer's discretion if you can stay or not. Many employers make their judgements on professional grounds, that is true, but there are actually those who do not. There are proposals from the opposition in this report to further impair employment security in the areas I have mentioned. There is even a reservation in which you want probationary contracts that are twice as long as today – from six months to twelve months: one year! I remember extending the probationary period from one month to three months in the early 1990s. There was a outcry in the trade union movement. Couldn't the employer after a month see if it was this person you should have or not? It was three months. We thought it was too long. Now we have six months. You're really gonna have to have a year? Then it is doubtful whether you are at all competent to make assessments in employment. Why, then, does this want to be done? Well, you say you want to lower the thresholds. The aim is to lower labour market thresholds. I would ask everyone to light the red warning light every time you hear a bourgeois say a reduced threshold. It is a different concept for the detriment of workers. Either you want to cut wages or you want to lower job security. Usually it's both. It is these reductions that are meant. The thresholds for a decent working life with decent conditions and therefore a good life are higher. Don't think anything else. That is not the path that we Social Democrats are going to take. We believe in strong rights for people at their jobs. We know that it creates safe people. We also know that strong and secure people make a democracy alive and a country stable. That is the society we want to build. That is also why, in just a few weeks' time, we have put forward two proposals that strengthen job security and the position of employees in the labour market. We want to develop the Swedish model. We don't want to lower it. I'm proud of that. Mr President, I would like to There are 24 reservations in the report. I wasn't going to point out any of them specifically, but now reservation 6 from the Alliance has discussed t s part. I'm having a hard time interpreting it at the moment. After suggestions have been made on how to change the Posting of Workers Directive in order to strengthen the conditions for a reasonable labour market for employees in this country, I find it difficult to read the reservation that exists, where you say that the government should not put its weight on this but on other things. Does the Alliance mean that, at this point, when a proposal from the Commission has come, the government will stop caring about this issue? Or what do you mean? After all, we will not go back to what it looked like before this reservation was probably written. I could read it, because it feels a little out of date.
